  1. I have encountered this problem several times.
  2. I encountered this problem several times.
  What is difference between sentence 1 and 2?
    ↳ **@jamesvanderhoorn1117** (👍1 · 2015-08-12) — https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=6IilS4SEqyA&lc=UgihMNEacLcQG3gCoAEC.7-H0Z7-CZQe70jrK-X4bE-
      Sentence 1 is what you'd normally expect. Someone is talking about his experience in the past up till now and saying there have been several occasions where he encountered that problem. Sentence 2 could be correct, if you want to imply that all those instances of running into that problem were in some definite period IN THE PAST, so not UP TILL NOW. For instance: I encountered this problem several times (when I was in primary school).

  Idioms in English - "Money" 
  - TIME IS MONEY= time is very important
  - PUT YOUR MONEY WHERE YOUR MOUTH IS= if something very important to you, put money down / invest.
  - MONEY BUMS A HOLE IN MY POCKET= wasting the money.
  - MONEY MAKES THE WORLD  GO ROUND= money is important, if you don't have money you can't do anything.
  - SHAKE YOUR MONEY MAKER= have fun.
  - MONEY TALKS= money is important.
  - MONEY IS THE ROOT OF ALL DEVIL= money is the cause of all the problems.

  what about past perfect
    ↳ **@Tokumei._.Hanako** (👍0 · 2021-08-12) — https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=TYPdBpkMl4I&lc=Ugy9phaUbLHpIHWLm6B4AaABAg.9K7nPnusaOZ9KSUGtDZq54
      The past perfect refers to a time earlier than before now. It is used to make it clear that one event happened before another in the past. It does not matter which event is mentioned first - the tense makes it clear which one happened first.  
      
      
      Had met: She had met him before the party. Had left: The plane had left by the time I got to the airport. Had written: I had written the email before he apologized.

      Do not worry this sentence is correct, dans he is a teacher after all we can trust him but I am sure that it is correct. :)
    ↳ **@katiefoley7046** (👍0 · 2015-08-12) — https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=loE2_ztqQ64&lc=UgjT9EECkw4ocXgCoAEC.7-H0Z7-Opwv78KgVJLEGCf
      This is very difficult to explain but yes it is correct. You are talking about when to use the verbs 'say', 'tell', 'talk' and 'speak'. 
      
       SAY is used with direct and indirect speech for instance: She said that it was my last chance.
      Say is most often used without a personal object. for instance: He said, ‘Good morning.’
      If we want to put a personal object after say, we use 'to' of instance 'When I say to you that I am honest, I am honest".
      
      TELL is used with direct and indirect speech
      after  the word tell, we usually say who is told, i.e., tell someone something for instance: She told me that she would be late and She told me that it was my last chance (not He told them, ‘Good morning.’)
      Tell is only used to mean ‘instruct’ or ‘inform’ for instance: 
      Tell is used to tell someone to do something for instance :Tom’s mother told him to clean his room.
      Tell is not used before objects like a word, a name, a sentence, a phrase 
      We do not usually use it after tell to refer to a fact for instance: I’ll tell you tomorrow.’ (not I’ll tell you it tomorrow.) 
      
      TALK
      There is not very much difference between speak and talk.
      Talk is the more usual word to refer to conversational exchanges and informal communication, for instance: When she walked into the room everybody stopped talking.
      
      SPEAK
      Speak is often used for one-way communication and for exchanges in more serious or formal situations for instance: I’ll have to speak to that boy -- he’s getting very lazy.
      Speak is the usual word to refer to knowledge and use of languages for instance: After she had finished reading the letter, nobody spoke.
      She speaks three languages fluently.

**@licuti5614** (👍0 · 2017-08-12) — https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=loE2_ztqQ64&lc=UgiRsrzDIGmw2HgCoAEC
  Dear James, I completely disagree with you. 
  Eg. He made her cry. 
  There are two clauses in the above sentence: Finite clause (He made) and non-finite clause (her cry). You should read more about modern linguistics.
    ↳ **@valentinaespinosa7894** (👍0 · 2018-08-12) — https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=loE2_ztqQ64&lc=UgiRsrzDIGmw2HgCoAEC.8NQ--W8DqN58WDLEaKlP-Z
      Bùi Ngọc Linh No, the clause doesn't stop at make because it wouldn't make sense. Make is a monotransitive verb in that example so it needs both the indirect object and direct object there to be an independent clause.
    ↳ **@syubidubidu3193** (👍0 · 2018-08-12) — https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=loE2_ztqQ64&lc=UgiRsrzDIGmw2HgCoAEC.8NQ--W8DqN58aiTio_3Cm_
      Pronoun cannot stand by its own. Hence it cannot make it into a new clause, because 'her' here functions as the object not a subject.

  I have been used to waking up at 7 am for 1 year etc. With the expression, 'I used to...' you always use an infinitive after it, i.e. I used to wake up at 6 am; I didn't use to wake up at 6 am (conjugates like a normal verb). You always use, 'I used to in the simple past or past perfect simple tenses, i.e. I used to go swimming (verb 'to go' is in the infinitive, while swimming is a gerund); I had used to go swimming before I started running.

**@MyChristopherD** (👍0 · 2012-08-12) — https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=ulezklvGaQk&lc=UgyPiD8xMJW6_rts3mR4AaABAg
  In a sentence, yes: 'I had been used to using running as a means to improving breathing capacity, but I now I am starting to become used to going swimming as a means to improving it'; In a parallel clause (or list), no: I used to run, swim and walk... (not I used to run, swim and walking...) Basically, with the expression, 'I am used to...' you always use a gerund after it (the tense doesn't matter), i.e I am used to waking up at 6 am; I was used to waking up at 7 am;

  i would like you explain about the difference between be used to and get used to
  i can't understand it.
    ↳ **@KpopKnitter** (👍2 · 2015-08-12) — https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=ulezklvGaQk&lc=UgiR5p3Adh3Xa3gCoAEC.7-H0Z7-5A_j70e-PpZX7MZ
      As James explained "used to" is something that you did in the past. "Get used to" means that you could do something regularly. For example, if I say "I could get used to making a lot of money", it means something like "I am starting to make more money and it is something that I would like to do/ or have happen regularly". Basically, "get used to" means that something is becoming a normal process or thing in your life. Let's look at another example. If I said "I got used to waking up early for school". This sentence means something like "I did not like waking up early before, but now that I have to, it has become a normal thing in my life". I hope this helps!

**@midnight15086** (👍4 · 2015-08-12) — https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Xp2dEM1jJOM&lc=UggGImAYpn6dTXgCoAEC
  I find it interesting when thinking about handshakes here in the U.S.-  We shake hands a lot when meeting or departing from someone we've met for the first time.  It's just a nice polite thing.  But I think it would actually be kind of awkward if you tried to shake hands with a very close friend.  They would be like, What are you doing?  We've known each other for years!  Lol.  I never see friends shakes one another's hands, only ppl who who have just been introduced.  Not that we hug alot either though... we hug when we see someone the first time after a long while, or when we won't be seeing them again for a long while.  Or when they're sad and we feel sorry for them (if we're close to them).  But when we meet up with ppl we see often, we hardly do anything at all.  We mostly just say "What's up?"  Haha.

  Most of japanese like to talk about person's feeling.
  Like this video, you say "it rains", Japanese say "hai". It is because they wait next sentence on person's feelings.
  For example if you say "It rains. It is good for us if it stops raining when we go outside.", Japanese will say various words.
  Japanese is always thinking about other's feeling.

**@mattdobz** (👍15 · 2015-08-12) — https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=tUrTT9I9wXA&lc=UgiUFP_VYTgLtHgCoAEC
  Proper nouns are awkward. If you have to use an apostrophe like the plural sense because a singular word ends in 's', you pronounce the word as if it doesn't have an apostrophe (e.g. James' pen would be said as "James pen", not "James-is pen"). If you need to indicate that you are saying James' (/d͡ʒeɪmz/) and not James (/ˈd͡ʒeɪms/), you subtly prolong and constrict the 's' to create a buzz similar to a 'z'. Adding an -is suffix to the pronunciation makes it more difficult to say (try saying "Jesus-is disciples" multiple times very quickly). This rule does apply to some regular nouns.
  
  When using "it's", you are using the shortened form of "it is" (e.g. it's a lovely day). To indicate possession to "it", the apostrophe is removed (e.g. a lion and its cubs).
    ↳ **@M_JackOfAllTrades** (👍1 · 2023-08-12) — https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=tUrTT9I9wXA&lc=UgiUFP_VYTgLtHgCoAEC.82h36fzIAIw9gaFZLepoHL

  I would say and write James' pen (ending in one s sound which is voiced with more of a z sound), but, I would say and write Chris's (Chris-iz) pen. I think many UK-English speakers, mostly  over the age of 35 , do this. To me, it is all to do with whether the s at the end of the word that is 'possessing' has a vowel "sound" immediately before the ending s. James has no vowel "sound" immediately before the s - but Chris does. So Chris's book -  however, James' book.

      ... and yes, I would say and write Jesus's disciples with two s sounds at the end! (Jesus-iz)

  You are great teacher! I'd like to ask something! In this two sentences: "Dogs are men's best friends."  and  " The men's car.", Why Do'nt we use "the" in the first one? Is there any rule to use it? Thanks by attention!!!!!!!
    ↳ **@KoniShadow** (👍2 · 2020-08-12) — https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=tUrTT9I9wXA&lc=UgyS-caAuEEJNZDKe8Z4AaABAg.8usAZf2-9jt91irNhZIg-r
      When you speak in a general way you cannot use "the", it is only used when you are referring to specific things like some people or some colors. "The men's car" means that you are talking about a group of men, no men in general. "Dog's are men's best friends" you are talking about all men, not a group of men.
